# G O D

## is the purest Love.

<note>The title "God is the purest Love" (original: "God is de reinste Liefde") was famously associated with a prayer book by Karl von Eckartshausen, which was widely translated and used across Europe during the Catholic Enlightenment. It reflects a shift toward a more emotional and personal relationship with the divine.</note>

### UPON AWAKENING.

Now I feel once again that I live: the night is over; I awake. I will therefore raise the first of my thoughts to You, all-good God! — to You, who gave me life, and watched over me while I slept.

O, how good You must be — You, who takes care of the smallest of your creatures! Good You must be: this thought shall never depart from my heart; the thought that You are good. How comforting it is for me! With what joy do I now behold the world again! — everything reminds me of your goodness, your love.

I am your creature, a handful of dust <note>A reference to the biblical account of creation in Genesis, where man is formed from the dust of the earth.</note>; but animated by your <term>Omnipotence <gloss>original: "Almagt" — the quality of having unlimited or very great power, specifically regarding God.</gloss></term>. I perceive a heart within me that has feeling; a soul, that for the

To whom, then, shall I offer the firstfruits <note>In this context, 'firstfruits' refers to the very first thoughts and emotions of the day, offered as a spiritual sacrifice.</note> of my feelings, but to You, <term>Incomprehensible Being <gloss>original: "onbegrijpelijk Wezen" — a theological term for God, whose nature exceeds the limits of human reason.</gloss></term>! who created me? — You are a Spirit, and I am a mortal. I can never comprehend You, yet I can still feel who You are. Love is your essence. Yes, You are love: for all your works proclaim love. The rising sun declares your majesty, and the joyful feeling of this morning your goodness. Today I see my Father again — my spouse — my children — my friends. They are healthy and hasten once again joyfully into my arms. Who took care of these loved ones while I slept? Who protected them against the dangers of the night? — You, <term>God of love <gloss>original: "God der liefde"</gloss></term>! You alone! And would my heart not love You? . . .

But — what does this mean: to love You? That I wish to ponder.

On this solemn morning, Creator of all beings! I will therefore make the most solemn promise before You; I will promise that your holiest examples shall be the <term>guiding principle <gloss>original: "rigtſnoer" — literally a 'plumb line' or 'rule' used to ensure a straight path or construction.</gloss></term> of my life. I will assist the wretched, take in the orphan, clothe the naked, heal the wounded, and guide the wandering back to the right path. I will be gentle toward my <term>subordinates <gloss>original: "onderhoorigen" — this refers to servants, laborers, or anyone under the speaker's social or professional authority.</gloss></term>; affectionate toward those whom Nature has led into my arms; indeed, everyone who is created in your <term>image <gloss>original: "evenbeeld"</gloss></term>, I will regard as my brother — as a child who is dear to You.

...feel. <note>The word "feel" (Dutch: "voelen") likely completes a word from the previous page, such as "medevoelen" (to empathize) or "medegevoelen" (sympathy).</note> For you know me well. Sincerely, like a child, I wish to walk with You — with You, who searches the <term>depths of my soul <gloss>original: "binnenste mijner ziele" — a phrase denoting the most intimate, internal part of a person's spirit.</gloss></term>. Accordingly, in these first hours of the day, I wish to give You the first proofs of my love. For Your sake, I shall forgive every one of my enemies; and I promise You, Lord! that I shall strive to do good to him who does me evil, to bless him who curses me, and to love him who hates me.

Therefore, I resolve never to speak ill of my <term>neighbor <gloss>original: "naasten" — referring to any fellow human being, derived from the biblical command to love one's neighbor.</gloss></term>, to give no wrong interpretation to his words and deeds, not to mock him, and not to be harsh toward him when he commits a <term>misstep <gloss>original: "misstap" — an error, lapse in judgment, or minor sin.</gloss></term>. I resolve to set him no bad example, never to be unjust toward him, never to mislead or deceive him; but always to act toward him as You have commanded me. I will not envy him when You bless him; nor be stingy when he needs my support. If I cannot help him by my <term>means <gloss>original: "middelen" — referring here to financial or material resources.</gloss></term>, I will give him good counsel and guidance. And all this I wish to do, because these are Your holy commandments, and because I love You.

# MORNING OFFERING.

The joyful morning smiles at me,
I am weary of the intoxicating <term>bed <gloss>original: "leger" — literally a lair or couch, used here poetically for a bed.</gloss></term>.

# REFLECTION
### ON THE
# DESTINY OF MAN.

Great and noble is our destiny, O Humans! We stand in the <term>chain of all things <gloss>original: "keten aller dingen" — a reference to the 'Great Chain of Being,' a hierarchical structure of all matter and life, thought in the 18th and 19th centuries to be decreed by God.</gloss></term> on the highest step of the <term>material world <gloss>original: "stoffelijke wereld."</gloss></term>. We already border the Angels, and the feeling of our soul elevates us to the rank of Spirits, although the heavy mass of the body presses us down toward the earth. Almost infinite are the powers that lie within us; infinite the paths along which we <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note> our higher desti-

<meta>catchword: -ny</meta>

<note>original vocabulary: Father, Angels, Spirits, destiny, material world</note>

<summary>This page concludes a morning hymn of praise and begins a philosophical reflection on the "Great Chain of Being," positioning humanity as the bridge between the physical world and the angelic realm.</summary>
<keywords>divine fatherhood, morning prayer, human destiny, Great Chain of Being, spirituality vs materiality, anthropology</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from page 6, completing the word "desti-ny" (bestemming) and expanding on the philosophical view of human nature as a bridge between the animal and the divine.</meta>

-ny <note>The word "destiny" is completed from the previous page's final word "desti-".</note> approach it—perfect ourselves—become like God. Everything proclaims to us that we are but wanderers here below, and he who feels his destiny does not linger on this journey. No, without stopping, he continues on the path that <term>Providence <gloss>original: "Voorzienigheid" — the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power.</gloss></term> marked out for him, and awaits the signal of the <term>Eternal One <gloss>original: "Eeuwigen" — a common Enlightenment-era reference to God.</gloss></term>.

According to the body, we are animals; according to the soul, immortal spirits. <term>Sensuality <gloss>original: "zinnelijkheid" — here meaning the state of being governed by physical senses and worldly desires rather than the spirit.</gloss></term> and decay are the lot of the shell that surrounds the soul; freedom and <term>immortality <gloss>original: "onsterfelijkheid."</gloss></term> are what await our spirit. The more man remains clinging to the material, the more he is an animal; the more man raises himself above the material, the more he approaches the Angel. Every step he takes forward or backward changes his position. From this come the thousands upon thousands of steps from the most imperfect to the most perfect; from the man who is more animal, to the man who is more spirit; from that which constitutes the distance from animal to man, and from that which turns the latter from man to angel.

Progress toward higher <term>perfection <gloss>original: "volmaaktheid."</gloss></term> is something good,

<meta>signature: A 4</meta>
<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of humanity, describing the body as animalistic and the soul as angelic, while urging the reader to progress toward divine perfection.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, dualism, soul vs body, Providence, immortality, spiritual progress, Great Chain of Being</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection from page 7, moving from the definition of human progress toward the specific role of divine love and virtue in achieving our ultimate purpose.</meta>

is something essentially good; and this essential, true good is our <term>destiny <gloss>original: "bestemming" — the ultimate purpose or divine goal of human life.</gloss></term>. <term>Religion <gloss>original: "Godsdienst."</gloss></term> and <term>revelation <gloss>original: "openbaring" — truths revealed by God to humanity.</gloss></term> teach us to know this destiny more closely. To be virtuous means to shape oneself more after the <term>Divinity <gloss>original: "Godheid."</gloss></term>, to approach more closely to the destiny of man, and to ascend further toward the unity of the whole.

God is the most perfect, the purest love; His highest <term>bliss <gloss>original: "zaligheid" — a state of supreme spiritual happiness and peace.</gloss></term> consists in endlessly active love; His <term>ordinances <gloss>original: "instellingen" — the laws or systems established by God.</gloss></term> are love, and our <term>calling <gloss>original: "beroep" — our spiritual vocation or duty.</gloss></term> is love. Simple and artless are His ordinances. "Love me; love one another:" this is His commandment. Oh, if only we recognized these commandments, how blessed this <term>lower world <gloss>original: "benedenwereld" — a common Enlightenment-era term for the earthly, mortal realm as opposed to the heavens.</gloss></term> would then be! — But alas! darkness still removes us ever further from the light; hatred and enmity still rob us of the pleasures of love. As soon as men love one another mutually, the world is immediately happy. But they do not love one another; they act as if they hated one another; they desecrate their destiny, and lower themselves to the level of predatory animals.

But let me not dwell further upon this; very few people understand me anyway. Those who do understand me,

### THE FEELING OF MY
## PRESENCE.

I feel my presence; that is to say: I feel that I live; I feel that I am a being who is conscious of itself. My eye sees the beauty of Nature — the flowering plains, the greening groves, the majestic forests. My ear hears the rustling of the brooks, the notes of the lark, the songs of the nightingale. I smell the fragrant flowers; I feel the scented breath of the roses; I perceive the coolness of the gentle western breeze on my hot cheeks and in my locks. Here these branches, laden with fruit, promise me food and refreshment. In the enjoyment of these, I notice a pleasant <term>sensation <gloss>original: "prikkeling" — literally a tingling or stimulation of the senses.</gloss></term> on my tongue. Yonder, the cherry and the pear invite me; a little further, the peach and the apricot. I want

<summary>This page marks a transition from theological reflection to a sensory celebration of existence, where the author explores self-awareness through the beauty and bounty of the natural world.</summary>
<keywords>consciousness, sensory experience, nature, divinity, Enlightenment thought, self-awareness</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, where the narrator began expressing their physical agency and the sensory delights of the natural world.</meta>

to go, and I can go; I want to pluck the fruits, and I am able to pluck them. I find <term>delight <gloss>original: "wellust" — in the 18th and early 19th century, this term referred to intense pleasure or aesthetic joy rather than its modern sexual connotation.</gloss></term> in the enjoyment, and that which is conscious of this delight, this feeling, that is me: — I — a riddle to myself; something that exists now, and once did not exist. I am, I feel it, that I am: but who brought me forth? Who set me down here? Who gave me this <term>wonderful frame <gloss>original: "heerlijke maaksel" — referring to the physical body as a divine masterpiece.</gloss></term>? Who gave me that susceptibility for the high enjoyment of everything that exists within me and around me? Who then is the being to whom I owe all this? — Him, who has made heaven and earth, and continues to care for everything so lovingly. — To Him I owe all of this! — 

But what are the joys of external Nature, compared to the feeling that He poured into my heart? . . . If I were to be present here all alone, oh! how quickly even the most beautiful joys of Nature would disgust me! His wisdom foresaw this; His love could <note>The author suggests that God's love would not permit the human soul to suffer the "disgust" of total isolation.</note> permit this. Therefore He gave me thousands of fellow creatures, in every way equal to myself, and poured a higher feeling into my heart, the feeling of <term>sympathy <gloss>original: "deelneming" — literally "participating" or "taking part in" the experiences of others.</gloss></term>, which rises within me when I see my fellow human beings in joy or in suffering.

### GOD IS LOVE.

Almighty Being, who created me! to whom I owe my existence! upon You I wish to reflect.

You are! all of Nature preaches Your presence to me. it is GOD. Yes, God! the God of all mankind, — the God of my parents <note>original: "ouderen" - while this can mean "elders" today, in this 18th-century context it specifically refers to one's parents</note>; the God of my brothers, — of my friends, — of my loved ones. You must truly mean well for us, just as a father means well for his children: — thus, yes, thus You must mean for us! Entirely pure love You must be. Yes, that You are, — entirely pure love. — — —

But what does this actually mean — love? . . . This is a question which I wish to answer from my own inner feeling <term>zelfsgevoel: a central concept in 18th-century Dutch philosophy, referring to the immediate, subjective awareness of one's own emotional state and existence</term>. An inclination, a striving, to see them lastingly happy, — to live entirely for them, — to exist for them completely; — to seek happiness, and to share it with them. Such sensations I had, thus I felt as a Human Being.—

Under the word *fear* <note>original: "vreeze" — in this context, the author is distinguishing between "servile fear" (dread of punishment) and "filial fear" (the desire not to disappoint a loved one).</note> one always understood a sort of aversion. I still remember very well that I felt fear for bad people—people who treated me poorly. Such a fear cannot, after all, be the fear of God. For God is love, and can one tremble before love? — No, the fear of God is a completely different feeling. The anxiety of acting against the will of the one whom one loves—this is a pure fear. To fear that which is contrary to divine love, that is pure, holy fear; that is the fear of love. Thus I wish to fear You, my God! In this way, and no other. You are not like the <term>Great of the earth<note>A common 18th-century reference to powerful monarchs or worldly aristocrats who ruled through fear and absolute authority.</note></term>, who like to see slaves at their feet, trembling at their commands. Your relationship to us is the same as that of a father to his children; love and trust form the chain by which You bind humanity to Yourself.

Forgive me, my God! that I have learned to know You so late. It was not my fault. People depicted You to me as being entirely different from how You truly are. When I was still a child, they called to me

### THE OFFENSE OF GOD.

Often people spoke to me of *offending*; of *offending God*. This is a weighty subject: I must therefore pause here for a few moments, so that no wrong ideas lead me astray. I can still remember the time very well in which I believed that one could <term>offend<note>original: "beleedigen" — to insult, wound, or wrong someone. The narrator is grappling with whether a perfect being can actually be "hurt" by human actions in the same way a person is.</note></term> You, my God, just like a human being. I looked upon You like a Lord, who [gives] orders to <meta>The sentence continues on the next page; the catchword "zijn" suggests the phrase "his subjects."</meta>

<summary>The narrator reflects on their spiritual growth, viewing divine discipline as a sign of love rather than malice, and begins to question the common belief that humans can "offend" God in the same way they insult one another.</summary>
<keywords>divine discipline, love of God, Father of mankind, forgiveness, offense of God, spiritual growth, anthropomorphism</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page, where the narrator compared God to a worldly master. It explores the etymology of "offending" God to shift the definition from a legalistic insult to a rejection of divine love and personal happiness.</meta>

[gives to] his household, and considers himself insulted by their transgression, and lashes out in wrath. But these were not the right concepts. You are no human; the offense done to You is not equal to that which one does to a human. This subject is weighty, very weighty. I must consider this matter maturely.

What do people call *offending God?*

To transgress Your commandments—to sin: this is, according to the general concept, offending God. Yet where does the word *offend* come from? — Actually, it means to bring someone some kind of <term>sorrow<note>original: "leed" — This is the root of the Dutch word "beleedigen" (to offend). The narrator is using this linguistic connection to argue that sin is not an insult to God’s ego, but a cause of "grief" to His loving nature.</note></term>. Therefore, when I transgress God's commandments, I do something which is a sorrow to God. But what does this latter part mean? — I must not imagine You as I imagine a human, but as what You indeed are—as a God, as the purest love. You love us humans. Your commandments are therefore the objectives of Your love—measures for our happiness. Thus, when I do not follow those commandments, I act against Your objectives, I thwart Your attempts to make me happy, and in so doing, I bring (if I may express myself this way) sorrow to Your love.

<vocab>God, to offend, commandments, sin, love, happiness</vocab>

<summary>The narrator deconstructs the concept of "offending God," arguing that because God is pure love, sin is not a personal insult to Him but rather a rejection of the happiness He intends for humanity.</summary>
<keywords>divine nature, etymology of offense, commandments, human happiness, nature of sin, God as love</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of "offending" God by moving from abstract definitions to a concrete, relatable analogy of a father and child. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on how sin is a cause of sorrow (leed) to God rather than an insult to His ego.</meta>

How can I form a <term>more sensory understanding<note>original: "zinnelijker begrip" — An understanding based on tangible experience or relatable human emotion rather than abstract theory.</note></term> of this? Come! I wish to look into my own heart. I love my child, and for that reason, I desire everything that can make them happy. Now I say: "My child! You must not eat of this fruit; that apple is poisonous; it would harm you." The child eats from the apple regardless, and finds death in it. 

Now my child acts against the aim of my love, and thwarts the measure that I employed for the promotion of their happiness. To feel how deeply such a thing causes <term>sorrow<note>original: "leed" — The author is linking this back to the root of the Dutch word for "offend" (beleedigen), suggesting that to offend God is to cause Him grief.</note></term> to a parent, one must be a father. In this way my child, through the transgression of the divine commandments, offends divine love. 

Yes, Lord! it is Your love which we offend when we transgress Your commandments. But You do not rave, You do not lash out in wrath against us if we do not obey Your orders. Those tender feelings, which the Divinity <note>The author uses "the Divinity" (de Godheid) as a synonym for God, common in late 18th-century Enlightenment-influenced spiritual writing.</note> planted in your heart for your sustenance and for your happiness, spread those also through your diligence over your fellow creatures; conduct yourself according to them: what is good for you, spread that also among others; what causes you pain, do not do that to others either. ### PRAYER FOR MY ENEMIES.

Lord! teach me to know the greatness of Your Divinity; teach me that a pure heart is the most beautiful gift, that repentance is the best sacrifice of atonement <note>original: "zoenoffer" — a theological term for an offering made to reconcile with God or make amends for wrongdoing.</note>, and that brotherly love is the best gift that one can bring to You, Infinite One! I promise You that I shall not seek to harm those who sought to harm me, nor will I curse those who cursed me.

### ON THE TRUE AND THE GOOD.

When I look around, O God! behold Your beautiful creation, and contemplate Your wise ordinances, then everything cries out to me that truth and goodness are the pillars upon which heaven and earth rest.

<meta>This page continues the meditation "On the True and the Good" from page 47. The author explores the metaphysical relationship between the human intellect (understanding) and the human heart (will), arguing that truth and goodness are inseparable aspects of God.</meta>

It is necessary then, O Lord! that I know what is true and good; and upon this weighty subject I wish now to reflect.

You alone are truth and goodness. Love within knowledge is the good, and love in practice is the true. <note>The author suggests that "the good" is the internal state of loving what is right, while "the true" is the external expression or exercise of that love.</note>

Truth and goodness must be united: for truth is an object of knowledge, and goodness an object of the will: — and what would knowledge be without the will?

The understanding and the wisdom of Your Angels, O Lord! arise through the union of the true with the good: without this union, there is only error and deceit.

Truth, O God! is what You are, and true is everything that You are; if I then seek truth, I must seek You, and become conformed to You.

You gave me will and understanding: — the understanding, to know; the will, to desire that which I have come to know. You, my God! are goodness; everything that has an existence is good, and everything is true which approaches the exercise of this goodness. If I know Your goodness, and this know-

He defines God as the ultimate source of both, asserting that seeking truth is synonymous with seeking God.</summary>

### KNOWLEDGE OF THE ETERNAL.
->*Human Calling and Human Duty.*<-

When I reflect upon myself, my God! then I feel that I have a body and a soul. The external, the body, is necessary for my present condition as a human; the internal, the soul, leads me to my future state as a spirit. Let me, O Infinite One! rightly consider this truth.

The senses connect me with the world; the properties of my soul with You, O God! I know

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues from the catchword on the previous page, "Ik" (I), completing the thought regarding the narrator's self-reflection.</meta>

therefore my calling: I do not exist to enjoy only through the senses like an animal; my destiny is higher on the ladder of beings. <note>The "ladder of beings" refers to the *scala naturae* or Great Chain of Being, a traditional philosophical concept that ranks all forms of life and existence from the lowest minerals to the divine.</note>

You are goodness, and the activity of goodness is love. — My destiny is to become like You; therefore my effort is to love as You love.

Your love is pure; so may my love also be pure like yours. I must therefore love the good because it is good, and the true because it is true: not for specific ulterior motives; not for the advantages that accompany the good; not out of vanity or ambition; but because the good is good, and the true is true. — What can prevent me from practicing such a pure love? Nothing but my self-love <note>In this historical context, "self-love" (*zelfsliefde*) is used negatively to mean egoism or selfishness, rather than modern self-esteem.</note>. I want to learn to know this, so that I can fight and oppose it, so that it does not hinder me on the path that leads to You, the All-Good <note>original: "Algoede"</note>.

I love myself when I grant good only to myself and not to others, or to them only for my own benefit; I love myself when I do good for the sake of honor, fame, and the applause of the world.

so that I may know who my neighbor is, and how I must love him.

My neighbor is, in general, every human being, without regard for status, religion, or sphere of activity—I must therefore love all people as myself:—I must thus accustom myself to a skill of placing myself in the condition of the other; a dexterity to put myself in his place, to personify myself with him, so that my feelings become his own. — I must train my heart toward sensitivity <note>original: "gevoeligheid"; the capacity to feel for others</note>—toward sensitivity, not toward sentimentality <note>original: "fijmelarij"; refers to a superficial, affected, or overly pious emotionalism</note>.

### The guideline <note>original: "rigtſnoer"; literally a plumb line or yardstick used to ensure moral accuracy</note> according to which I must perform my acts of love consists of the following fundamental principles:

Put yourself in the place of your neighbor, and act toward him just as you wish that he would act toward you, if you were in his place.

Always prefer the present, urgent need of your neighbor over your own future needs; and do as much as you can for his rescue.

### PRAYER FOR KNOWLEDGE OF FAITH.

Lord, my Creator! let me know what faith is, so that I may worship You in truth. My internal perception of the true and my inclination toward the good must first give a substantial value to my faith. Just as truth and goodness must be united with one another, so must faith be united with works, for faith without works is dead. <note>This is a reference to the New Testament book of James 2:17: "Even so faith, if it hath not works, is dead, being alone."</note> — Lord! let me contemplate Your beautiful creation, for then it shall be an image to me of true faith.

When the light that comes from the sun is united with heat, then everything on the face of the earth flourishes; conversely, when light is without heat, then the beautiful soil withers, and everything sheds the glorious ornaments of nature. <note>original: "fieradiën"; an archaic Dutch term for decorations or adornments, used here to describe the blossoms and greenery of the natural world.</note>

May this image remind me of faith, which is light without heat if works do not make it alive. Thus, the spiritual light is the truth of faith, and the spiritual heat is love.

<vocab>Prayer, Faith, Creator, Works, Love, Spiritual light</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a prayer asking for a deeper understanding of faith, using the metaphor of sunlight and heat to argue that faith (light) is only life-giving when joined with love and good works (heat).</summary>
<keywords>faith and works, spiritual light, spiritual heat, solar analogy, creation, James 2:17, spiritual life</keywords>

### GOD’S OMNIPRESENCE.

Savior of mankind! when You wrestled with death, when Your soul raised itself to the Father and was deeply immersed in a holy prayer, when Your sweat and Your blood ran onto the earth, in that so solemn hour, then You made known that great truth—a truth that shall be and remain as long as the garment <note>original: "omkleedfel"; referring to the physical body as a temporary covering for the eternal soul</note> of the eternal soul is dust. You stood and said to those who slept: Your soul is indeed willing, but the flesh is weak! <note>A reference to Jesus’s words in the Garden of Gethsemane, found in Matthew 26:41.</note>

This fate of finitude, this gravity of the earth my soul also feels when it wishes to raise itself to You, O God! to You, the Infinite.

Worshiping, Father! I sink into the dust and weep! Hear my moaning, the voice of the finite! Fill my soul with Your Spirit, so that it may raise itself to You, to You!

<summary>The author concludes a prayer on faith and works before beginning a meditation on God’s omnipresence, reflecting on the human struggle to reach the divine despite the weaknesses of the physical body.</summary>
<keywords>omnipresence, Gethsemane, finitude, spiritual struggle, soul vs. flesh, Matthew 26:41, prayer</keywords>

### MEDITATION ON GOD'S WISDOM.

All-good God! When I contemplate Your wisdom, Your love, how worthy of adoration You are to me! In deep worship, I wish to meditate on who You are—wisdom and love!—Full of admiration, I bow before You in the dust, and find no words to exalt You; only my heart, only my feeling presses toward You—my God, my Lord, and my Father! Wherever I look, I see the works of Your love. Magnificently the Sun rises in the firmament, <note>original: "uitspansel"; a poetic or biblical term for the vault of the heavens.</note> majesty is her garment, <note>In the original Dutch, the Sun is feminine; this personification adds to the poetic, "warm" quality of the praise.</note> her gaze gladdens all living creatures, and this Sun is Your work, You God of glory and of strength!

The darkness of the night, the host of stars <note>original: "sterrenheer"; literally "star-army" or "star-host."</note> that shines over me, beckons me to deep adoration—everywhere I look, You are there; everything proclaims Your existence, everything Your greatness, Your love—everywhere is a temple of the Divinity.

Now the soft winds rustle, now the beneficial dew drips from heaven, now the rain pours itself into the lap of the earth, blessing us with

# THOUGHTS ON REDEMPTION

God! Can my heart even conceive the thought! — to think the thought of the highest love — the thought of redemption! — In a holy darkness <note>original: "heilig duister"; a reference to the "divine dark" or the concept that God's ways are beyond human light/reason.</note> I feel Your great work, without my understanding fathoming You — and how could the worm <note>A common 18th-century metaphor for human insignificance and humility in the presence of the Creator.</note> fathom You, who does not even know the wonders of the Sun, nor can determine its arrangement, and yet feels the blessing of its warmth in the sunlight — no, soul! — no! you cannot fathom the Divine, the high decree, when Jesus gave Himself as a sacrifice — you can only feel the most divine of love. O human wisdom! you who are foolishness in the eye of eternal wisdom — do not take from me the comfort that Christ <note>original: "Christus"</note> bore my guilt in the death of the cross; how terribly I would have to tremble, if God were not my Redeemer. No! this comfort, which faith gives me, no scoffer can rob from me; I feel its divinity, I adore and believe. I feel peace and rest in this holy faith — rest, which no

<term>Redemption (Verlossing): The theological concept of being saved from sin through the sacrifice of Jesus.</term> <term>God: The Divine Creator.</term> <term>Jesus: The central figure of the Christian faith.</term> <term>Christ: A title for Jesus, meaning "the anointed one."</term> <term>Faith (geloof): Trust or belief in the divine.</term> <term>Human wisdom (menschenwijsheid): Intellectual reasoning, which the author views as limited compared to divine truth.</term> <term>Cross (kruis): The instrument of Christ’s execution and a symbol of sacrifice.</term>

<summary>The author reflects on the mystery of divine redemption, arguing that while the human mind is too limited to "fathom" the mechanics of God's sacrifice, the soul can feel its warmth and find peace through faith.</summary>
<keywords>Redemption, Divinity, Jesus Christ, Faith vs. Reason, Humility, Salvation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the prayerful meditation on redemption from page 65. ### PRACTICE OF THE THEOLOGICAL VIRTUES. <note>The "Theological Virtues" are Faith, Hope, and Charity; they are considered gifts from God rather than habits acquired by human effort.</note>

#### FAITH.

I believe in **You**, Lord! eternal, true God! I believe that You are simple in nature <note>original: "éénvoudig in de natuur"; refers to the theological doctrine of Divine Simplicity, meaning God is not "composed" of different parts but is a unified essence.</note>, and threefold in persons: God the Father my Creator, God the Son my Redeemer, and God the Holy Spirit my Sanctifier! I believe that You are a rewarder of those who seek **You**, and that You recompense the good; but also, as a righteous Judge, You punish the evil. I believe all things firmly and steadfastly which the Catholic Church commands us to believe; and I believe all this specifically because You, my God—who are infinitely true and the Possessor of all perfections—have revealed such. In this true Catholic faith I wish to live and die; yes, I am prepared to surrender my life rather than deviate from this true faith in the slightest. Preserve me, O God! through Your grace, in this faith.

<vocab>Practice, Theological virtues, Faith, God the Father, God the Son, God the Holy Spirit, Catholic Church</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the "Practice of the Theological Virtues," specifically providing a formal "Act of Faith" wherein the believer affirms their commitment to the doctrines of the Trinity and the authority of the Church.</summary>
<keywords>faith, Holy Trinity, creed, Catholic doctrine, divine simplicity, salvation, theological virtues</keywords>

# HOPE. <note>This is an "Act of Hope," a formal prayer where the believer expresses trust that God will grant them eternal life and the means to achieve it.</note>

I hope, O my God! with full confidence in Your infinite mercy; for You alone, my God! are my comfort, my helper in all my needs of soul and body. I hope, through the infinite merits <note>original: "oneindige verdiensten"; refers to the spiritual "credit" or grace earned by Jesus through his life and death, which the believer can draw upon.</note> of Jesus Christ our Lord, Your only-begotten Son, to obtain the forgiveness of my sins with a firm confidence. I hope through these merits to receive the grace to live piously, to mourn my sins, to obtain their forgiveness and final perseverance in goodness, and thus to become a partaker <note>original: "deelachtig te worden"; to share in or participate in.</note> in eternal salvation. All this, O my God! I expect from You, because You have promised it, and You, being infinitely powerful, are able to give me that which You have pledged; and You, being infinitely good, also desire to give me what You have promised, and You are infinitely faithful in the fulfillment of Your promises. O infinitely powerful, O immeasurably good, O eternally faithful God! strengthen me in this, my hope!

<vocab>Hope, mercy, Jesus Christ, forgiveness, sins, grace, salvation, confidence</vocab>

<keywords>Hope, Act of Hope, mercy, salvation, Jesus Christ, divine grace, perseverance, theological virtues</keywords>

### LOVE AND CONTRITION. <note>In a religious context, "Love" here refers to "Charity" (Latin: *caritas*), the highest theological virtue, which involves loving God for His own sake and others for the sake of God.</note>

My God and my Lord! I love You with my whole heart, because You are the highest, infinite good, worthy of all love and honor above all things. With my whole soul, and with all my strength I love You, because You are most worthy in Yourself to be loved above all else. My neighbor <note>original: "Den Naasten"; in a biblical sense, this refers to any fellow human being, following the command to "love thy neighbor."</note>, and also my enemies, I love, and I forgive them, because they are Your image, and because my divine Teacher also loved His enemies on the cross. In this love I wish to live and die. O God! kindle in me the fire of Your love.

From the depths of this pure love, I regret with a contrite heart <note>original: "verbroken hart"; literally "broken heart," a traditional term for deep, spiritual sorrow for sin.</note> all the sins of my whole life, with which I have ever offended You, who are the most high, infinitely perfect, and most lovable good. I hate and detest them with the greatest aversion, and wish for nothing more fervently, my God, than that I had never committed them. I also pray to You for this reason, my God! hum- <note>The text ends with the fragment "oot-", which combines with the next page's catchword "moe-" to form "ootmoedig," meaning "humbly."</note>

### CONTEMPLATING THE OMNIPRESENT MAJESTY OF GOD.

Collect your thoughts, my spirit! prepare yourself to praise the eternal Father and sustainer of all beings! — Break the magic bonds with which the world has too long shackled you to trivial sensations. Far from creatures that rob you of close communion and union with the Creator, you must collect yourself, and eventually you must, completely free from all intoxication of the senses <note>original: "zinnelijkheid"; in this 18th/19th-century context, this refers to being distracted by physical or worldly desires rather than modern "sensuality."</note>, belong entirely to yourself. What am I before You! — How exhausted is mortal man by the first thought of Your <term>omnipresence [alomtegenwoordigheid]: the state of being present everywhere at all times</term>, Your <term>omnipotence [almagt]: the quality of having unlimited or very great power</term> and <term>glory [heerlijkheid]: the splendor and majesty of the divine</term>! (1) <note>The author refers to a state of "werkeloosheid," suggesting a return to the void or the helplessness of the human soul before it was animated by God.</note> I forget my existence, and am only still in You — through You in thoughts of You! (2) What were the world in which I live, this world full of wonders, if a breath of Your unchangeable power did not support its existence? (3) If You will, then the suns, which You have ignited, melt away. God! Who encompasses Your omnipotence? Who fathoms Your <term>eternity [eeuwigheid]: infinite time</term>? It is You, O <term>Omnipresent One [alomtegenwoordige]: the state of being present everywhere at all times</term>! who has ordered all these things, these millions of Beings, so perfectly. <note>The Dutch "onverbeterlijk" literally means "unimprovably," implying a state of perfection that cannot be increased.</note> **(2)** You call the winter, and already the earth grows old; already the plains hide themselves beneath their protectress, the woolly snow. ### PERCEIVING THE OMNIPRESENT
### WISDOM OF GOD.

Eternal light! (1) enlighten the spirit that You have created to know You, which seeks its glory and its <term>salvation [zaligheid]: a state of eternal happiness or blessedness in the presence of the Divine</term> in You. Scatter the mists that surround me and hide You from me, though You are present everywhere. Let just one ray, O God! just one ray of Your enlightenment fall into my inner self, so that the knowledge of Your mysterious ways may bring me closer to the goal of my destiny. What is the spirit of mortal man when he walks far from Your light in the valleys of death? (2) He seeks the essence and embraces only shadows; he grows dizzy upon the peaks of earthly wisdom. Hidden God! whom all Your creatures seek! Oh, how infinitely You are exalted above all our perceptions! — Sun of eternity! <note>original: "Zonder eeuwigheid." This is likely a transcription or typesetting variant for "Zon der eeuwigheid" (Sun of eternity), a common metaphor in 18th-century devotional literature.</note> Enlighten our original darkness into a beneficial twilight, in which we learn to know Your existence.

Pure and innocent You created the <term>forefather [stamvader]: a reference to Adam, the first human in the biblical tradition</term> of the human race. Ordained for immortality, he received everything from You that could make him pleasing to You. You adorned his mind with knowledge and freedom; You filled his heart with the longing for perfection; and thus You accustomed it to the voice of the known truth. --- Eternity! --- Thought without beginning and without end! how You move my entire heart! --- You are infinite as the <term>Divinity [Godheid]: the state or essence of being God</term>. In You my spirit loses itself --- and in You it finds its existence again without ceasing! I shall be eternal, God! I, Your finite creature --- shall be eternal! A thousand times a thousand million years shall pass, and still --- I shall think and live; and once again a thousand times a thousand million years shall swallow my existence, and still --- I shall not be a single moment closer to the end of my being! --- Spirit of eternity! --- Life of my spirit! ### PERCEIVING THE OMNIPRESENT
### GOODNESS
### OF GOD.

Spirit of creation! (1) Eternal union of omnipotence and wisdom! Original love! — To You my soul lifts itself! To You the creature approaches, who experiences the influence of Your goodness. (2) In the silence of solitude, far from the appearance of splendor that blinded me, free from the snares in which my heart often became entangled, I come closer to You. Beneficent Being! <note>The term "Weldadig Wezen" (Beneficent Being) reflects the Enlightenment-era view of God as a source of universal kindness and order.</note> How near are You to the spirit that seeks You, and how inseparable from the creatures who truly possess You! Source of omnipresent goodness! Where then do mortals find the rest for which their hearts long? Where do I find that pleasant satisfaction of that insatiable desire other than in You, the only Goal of beings? Only You still that longing, which the world cannot satisfy! —

What is this world, whose enjoyment tires me,

### WORSHIP, HUMILITY, AND TRUST.

My God, I can and may love You! Let me think this great thought, and send light into my soul! May the holy flame of Your love raise my spirit from the prison of sensuality <note>original: "zinnelijkheid" — refers to the state of being preoccupied with physical senses or worldly desires rather than spiritual matters.</note> to You, and fill it with Your divine fire. Tear my spirit loose for a few moments from the bonds of the senses, so that it may approach Yours, and worship You in truth!

Let me, Infinite One! feel Your presence! Feel Your holy presence; — feel that I breathe, live, and exist through You. The heart that beats in my chest, the blood that runs through my veins, moves and flows through Your goodness. Without You, my heart would no longer beat, my blood would cease its flow, and my spirit could no longer live. You are the life of all things; I live through You, and could not exist without You.

You are the unity, the power of all powers, through which everything works! — Work in me the work of sanctification! <note>original: "heiliging" — the process of being made holy or purified from sin.</note> raise my spirit, so that it may feel it is approaching You. Melt away the sensual covering,

In the future, may our heart and our conscience be pure, our conduct blameless, and our entire appearance modest, calm, and humble. To faithfully follow God's will, to do good in silence,

God pays heed to the innermost heart,

That is already prayer in the eyes of God.

### Offering of bread and wine.

God! the mixing of water with the wine is a symbol of the union of the Divine nature with the human, of our offering with the offering of the Savior. Lord! let me offer my heart to You as a sacrifice, and grant that just as the Priest unites the wine with the water, I may unite the knowledge of what is true with the practice of what is good.

### The Preface.
<note>original: ‘Præfatie’ — from the Latin ‘praefatio,’ meaning ‘introduction.’ This is the opening prayer of the Liturgy of the Eucharist, usually beginning with an invitation to "Lift up your hearts."</note>

Christian virtue is a readiness <note>original: ‘vaardigheid’ — here meaning a habitual disposition or practiced skill in moral living.</note> to think and act according to Jesus’ precepts and example. To be a Christian means, therefore: to follow Christ, to strive after His example, to be like Him in spirit and life! And this imitation consists in the earnest, active, and unceasing effort to correspond with Him—partly in His pious and virtuous mindset in general, and partly in His conduct toward

### *Adoration of the Most Holy Sacrament of the Altar, during the Afternoon Service.*

O my Lord and my God! O Jesus, God and man alike! I believe that which I do not see; I submit my senses and my reason to faith and the infallibility of Your holy word. ### PRAYER FOR HIS FRIEND AND BENEFACTOR.

God of love! To You I owe the gentle feeling of friendship and love. You placed it deep within my heart, and gave me the most blessed of human joys. <note>original: "menschenwellust." In the 18th and early 19th centuries, this term did not carry the modern connotation of "lust," but rather referred to a pure, intense rapture or delight available to humanity.</note> O Creator of human happiness! Pour out blessing and salvation upon him whom my soul loves! Preserve him from the dangers of life, and protect him, so that no sorrow may strike this rational being. <note>original: "redelijken." Literally "the rational one." This reflects Enlightenment-era theology, which emphasized the human capacity for reason as a divine gift.</note> May the sun rise bright over his head each day, and may his mouth give a contented smile at the twilight of evening. Give him gentle dreams when he sleeps, and pour joy into his heart when he awakes again in the morning. Guide him on the paths of life, and do not allow grief and worries to furrow his brow. May the tear of misery never glisten on his cheeks, and may sickness or poverty never weigh down his spirit. Bright as a beautiful spring morning be each of his days, and soft as a brook may his life flow as if through flowers

<vocab>Prayer, Friend, Benefactor, Creator, Blessing, Salvation/Well-being</vocab>

<summary>This page contains the beginning of a sentimental prayer asking for God's protection and favor upon a beloved friend and benefactor. The speaker expresses deep gratitude for the gift of friendship and petitions for their friend to have a life characterized by peace, health, and joy.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, friendship, gratitude, benefactor, divine protection, happiness, Enlightenment, emotionalism</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page ("...flow as if through flowers onward") and concludes the prayer for a friend and benefactor. The tone remains deeply sentimental, typical of late 18th or early 19th-century Dutch pietism, emphasizing God's personal care for the individual’s physical comfort and moral standing.</meta>

onward. Protect him, when misfortune threatens him, or enemies pursue him, so that no evil may befall him. Everywhere he walks, may he feel Your goodness and Your blessings. When the sweat of labor covers his brow, may the gentle west wind dry the droplets; when fatigue weighs down his body, may Your providence <note>original: "voorzienigheid." In this era, Providence was viewed as God’s active, protective hand guiding the daily lives of the faithful.</note> bring him under shade-giving trees, and prepare a bed of flowers for his resting place; when his tongue is dry from thirst, may the spring refresh his lips. — Give, my God! to him all the pleasures of life. Protect his soul against tempestuous passions, <note>original: "onstuimige driften." The Enlightenment-era ideal was to maintain a calm, rational soul; "passions" were seen as storms that could lead one astray from virtue.</note> and if he wanders out of weakness, bring him back gently to the paths of virtue, and punish him not, but spare him according to Your mercy. May Your Angel be with him in all the affairs of his life; may he be his guide, his protector, and unite us one day on the other side of the grave, where there is no more separation, but pure, holy love. Amen.

# PRAYER FOR THE DECEASED.

I remember, Lord! those loved ones who already slumber in the grave. Death closed their eyes; they are no more for me. Forgive, Lord! if human feeling forces a tear from my eye. In the eyes of Your goodness, God of love! the emotion of the soul is no crime. <note>original: "geene misdaad." This reflects a common theme in 18th-century devotional literature: the reconciliation of natural human grief with the religious duty of submission to God's will.</note>

Are they then no more for me, those whom I loved? Can no tear make their ashes live again? — No kiss breathe life into the dust into which their earthly vessel <note>original: "omkleedsel." Literally "covering" or "garment," a metaphor for the physical body as a temporary shell for the soul.</note> has fallen? — O God of love! strengthen my spirit, and instill comfort into my heart. — O yes, they are not lost to me, those beloved Departed! they have passed into Your bosom, Father of mankind! Why should I mourn? — I shall see them again — see them again on the other side, where there is no death, where there is no more separation. They are with You; where could they dwell better, my Father? — I pray for my brothers, who have laid aside this perishable garment <note>original: "vergankelijk bekleedsel." ### THANKSGIVING FOR IMMORTALITY (Continued)

...be. How shall I name You? My trembling lips fall silent. How shall I think of You? Even my soul stands still. No, my intellect cannot reach You. — Father! Father! Thus shall my lips stammer of You, my soul imagine You, my heart feel You. — — — Father! Father! Father! — — Oh, fall down and worship, You mortals! He is the Father of us all! — — Immerse yourself in contemplation, my soul! Beat, heart, with joy! — — You were created for immortality! Not in the rolling thunder, not in the roaring storm alone: also in the soft whispering of the west wind, all of nature proclaims Your <note>Here "Your" could refer to the Divine persistence or the soul's own endurance, a common ambiguity in 18th-century devotional literature.</note> persistence. How will it be for me! — O words of eternal life! There I sit now, by the first rays of morning, above the graves. Lovely morning! You bring me rest and peace! A new day proclaims new life to me. Alas, a thousand brothers lie dissolved in the dust beneath my feet. How were you <note>Addressing the deceased "brothers" buried below.</note> in your time? Was your heart also so restless? Was the human world also sometimes so empty, so inadequate, so unsatisfying to you? Oh, I wish that I were among you!

# P R A Y E R
### OF A MAN WHO FREQUENTS THE COURT.

You great, mighty God! — You King of Kings! Lord of Lords! — It pleased Your will that I should live at the Court, and Your holy will gave me the social station <note>original: "stand". In the 18th and 19th centuries, one's "stand" or "estate" was seen as a divinely appointed place in the social hierarchy with specific moral obligations.</note> in which I live: give me therefore also Your grace, that I may know the duties of my station, and faithfully fulfill them. Let me do as much good as is possible within the circle in which You have placed me; and let me not be blinded by the splendor of the Court, or by the worldly mindsets of men. Give me a sincere heart that loves the King and the Fatherland <note>original: "Vaderland". This term refers to the ancestral country and carries deep patriotic and moral weight.</note> without self-interest. Let me always seek the common good <note>original: "Gemeene best". Translated as the public interest or commonwealth; the collective well-being of society.</note> without ulterior motives, and strengthen my courage amidst my occupations. Preserve my heart, so that it does not allow itself to be blinded by gold, honorary posts, and false ambition, but enlighten my understanding, so that it may see that only that which deserves approval in Your eyes is truly great.

King: Koning
Fatherland: Vaderland
Common good: Gemeene best
Ambition: eerzucht
</vocab>

### PRAYER IN TIMES OF WAR.

I lift up my heart to You, Father of mankind! and pray to You for mercy and <term>forgiveness<note>original: "verschooning." This can also mean "sparing" or "exemption," suggesting a plea for God to spare the people from the horrors of war.</note></term>. Enlighten the hearts of men, that they may see that You are the God of love, not the God of wrath and of murder. Enlighten their hearts toward a universal love of humanity, and destroy the will—the passions of the <term>lust for conquest<note>original: "zucht naar verovering." A reference to the imperial ambitions or expansionist desires that fueled many 18th and 19th-century conflicts.</note></term>—from their souls. Give rest and peace again to the oppressed lands, and clear the understanding of the nations, so that justice, gentleness, and wise counsel may decide their rights, and no longer violence and murder. Amen.

### PRAYER AFTER HEARING THE WORD OF GOD.

Merciful God! I thank You for the grace of Your holy Word. Grant that I may be merciful, just as my Savior was also merciful! Let me remember that every drink of water I offer to the least of Your creatures is given to You! <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 25:40: "Truly I tell you, whatever you did for one of the least of these brothers and sisters of mine, you did for me."</note> Grant that I may reach out a hand to suffering virtue, and sow here that noble seed which shall bear fruit in heaven; that through righteousness and virtue, through teaching and example, through help and rescue, through warning and compassion, through comfort and friendship, I may again find mercy before You, Father of mankind! Grant that I may become conformed to Jesus Christ in all things through my conduct.

Take, Lord, the heart of stone from me, and give me a new, obedient, good, and <term>sensitive heart<note>original: "gevoelig hart." This refers to those who live according to reason and divine law, often used in Enlightenment-era Dutch theology to describe the virtuous believer.</note></term>, and strive with all my heart to become like him, and...

# EVENING PRAYER.

How shall I thank You, my God! for all the good that You have shown me this entire day? From where shall I find the feelings of gratitude that are worthy of Your great, undeserved goodness? — From where the words with which I could proclaim Your praise as it deserves? — I can say nothing else but: You are my Father, You are the God of love; but I am unworthy, and far too insignificant for all that mercy and faithfulness which You have shown me. Yes, I am not even worthy to call You Father. How much good have You shown me again this day, my God! — You have preserved me, You have granted me clothing and a home; thousands of disasters that could have struck me, You have turned away from me. But now it is only right that I also put this question to my heart: how have I spent this day? Whether I have become better, more pious, and more worthy of You? I must ask myself: whether I [have pursued] <term>Christian perfection<note>original: "Christelijke volmaaktheid." In this context, it refers to the 18th-century devotional ideal of striving for a complete moral and spiritual alignment with the life of Jesus, rather than just following basic rules.</note></term> just as much

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a formal evening prayer, focusing on the practitioner's deep gratitude for God's protection and physical provisions throughout the day. It concludes with a call to rigorous self-examination regarding one's spiritual growth and "Christian perfection."</summary>
<keywords>evening prayer, gratitude, divine providence, self-reflection, Christian perfection, piety, humility</keywords>

Lord! my helper and my God! I pray to You for the **Spirit of true earnestness** <note>original: "Geest des regten ernst." This refers to a state of sincere spiritual focus and moral sobriety required for honest self-examination.</note>, that it may not let me end this day without being better and more pleasing to You. <meta>This page continues the "Evening Prayer" sequence from page 204. Having asked for the "Spirit of true earnestness" to review the day's actions, the speaker now begins a detailed series of interrogative reflections addressed to God, moving from general sinful habits to specific physical and social behaviors.</meta>

Have I not fallen again today into those **sins** <note>original: "zonden." In this context, these are not just errors, but moral failings that separate the individual from the divine.</note> that are otherwise so habitual to me?

Have I always carefully and conscientiously guarded my **temperament** and my own heart?

Have I paid attention to the first **sensual inclination** <note>original: "zinnelijke neiging." This refers to physical or worldly desires—such as greed, lust, or gluttony—that distract from spiritual focus.</note>, and immediately sought to suppress it in Your fear, my God! and in faith?

Have I kept my senses and desires in check, and closed my heart to the **temptations** to sin?

Have I kept my eyes and ears closed to the inducements to sin? — sought no cause or opportunity to fulfill a sensual desire anywhere?

Was I **moderate** and **sober** <note>original: "matig en nuchteren." These virtues were central to 18th and 19th-century Dutch Reformed piety, emphasizing self-control over bodily appetites.</note> in food and drink? — Did I think then of You, the good Giver, and of my needy brothers? — Did I not misuse Your gifts?

Was I cautious in my speaking? Cautious in all my steps and strides? Or have I acted according to the whims and desires of my heart?

Have I allowed myself to be carried away to that which displeased You, and was not approved by my **conscience**?

Am I also fair enough to confess my failings to others who witnessed them and were offended by them? Or is it rather my plan, out of **self-love** <note>original: "eigenliefde." In this theological context, self-love is viewed as the root of pride, leading a person to prioritize their own reputation over honest repentance.</note>, to hide, excuse, and gloss over my irregular emotions and harmful impulsiveness?

I may answer these questions to myself reasonably or unreasonably, but You, my **Creator and Judge**! know the true state of the depths of my heart. If I hide my transgressions, my God, how can I then mourn them? How can I then find forgiveness from You? Therefore, O my God, I will confess my **sins** to You, and none of my **misdeeds** <note>original: "misdaden." ### MEDITATION ON DEATH
### AND PRAYER.

Why should I fear death, which brings me back into Your arms, You God of love! — Would the child tremble when he enters his Father's house? Would the infant weep when its mother takes it to her breast? Are You not the God of love? — the God of love, who hates none of His creatures <note>original: "schepselen." In this historical religious context, this refers to all living beings created by God, emphasizing their dependence on the Creator.</note>? And would I tremble when You call me? ### Christian Psalms.

#### First Psalm.

-> On God the Creator. <-

Praise, my soul, your Creator! for all his works are good, great, and wonderful!

The heavens and the earth, and all that they contain, He has made; — by His word He created them out of nothing; — everything proclaims His omnipotence, wisdom, and goodness.

He created man in His image, noble and good, and gave him an immortal soul, which should know Him, love Him, and become eternally blessed <note>original: "gelukzalig." This term carries a sense of supreme happiness or divine beatitude.</note>.

All animals on earth, in the air, and in the water He has made, and He gives them everything in abundance to live and sustain themselves.

The lilies in the field, the flowers in the garden He allows to grow and thrive.

From where comes the great, glorious Sun, which rises every morning over the good and the evil, and which everything <note>The sentence ends abruptly here and continues on the following page.</note>

### Second Psalm.
->Concerning God the Redeemer.<-

God so loved the world that He sent His own Son to us, so that all who know Him, believe in Him, follow Him, and might be saved <note>original: "zalig worden" — to be made blessed, blissful, or to attain eternal salvation</note> through Him.

Much has God’s Son, Jesus Christ, done for us! Much has He suffered, to seek and to save that which was lost.

As a child, He came into the world poor; but He was pious and good, obedient and submissive to His Parents.

As a youth, He was found in the Temple, in the house of His Father; He stayed among the Teachers <note>original: "Leeraars" — referring to the doctors of the law or rabbis in the Temple of Jerusalem</note>, and He also increased before God and men in wisdom as well as in years.

As a man, He began the work of redemption; sin was to be destroyed, virtue introduced, and fallen man restored, reconciled with God, to become God’s Friend, God’s child, and an heir of heaven.

<note>Vocabulary: Second Psalm, God the Redeemer, Jesus Christ, redemption, blessed/saved, sin, virtue</note>

<summary>This page introduces the "Second Psalm," focusing on the life of Jesus Christ. It traces His journey from a humble, obedient childhood to His youth in the Temple and His adult mission to redeem humanity and reconcile them with God.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Redeemer, Second Psalm, salvation, childhood of Jesus, Temple, redemption, virtue, reconciliation</keywords>

### *Third Psalm.*

->Of God the Sanctifier.<-

It was not enough that God created humanity, or that Jesus redeemed them; —— they also still had to be sanctified <note>original: "geheiligd" — to be made holy or set apart for God's service</note>.

This is the work of the Holy Spirit, whom Jesus so often promised to His disciples <note>original: "Jongeren" — literally "youths," but used here in the biblical sense of followers or disciples</note>.

Who on the day of Pentecost <note>original: "Pinksterdag" — the Christian feast commemorating the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the Apostles</note> came visibly over them, and taught, strengthened, and comforted them.

Whom the Apostles so often imparted to the first Christian people through prayer, and whom we also have received.

Yes! in holy Baptism I have been sanctified by God’s Spirit. He still sanctifies even now; through Him comes light and grace <note>original: "genade" — unmerited divine assistance given for regeneration or sanctification</note>, comes comfort and joy into our hearts.

Thanks be to You, O Spirit of God! for all the good that You bestow upon humanity! Ah! if only I might be completely filled by You!

<note>Vocabulary: Sanctifier, Holy Spirit, Pentecost, Apostles, Baptism, grace</note>

### Fourth Psalm.

->About God the righteous Judge.<-

God the Father has given His Son Jesus Christ all power in heaven and on earth.

He shall therefore one day judge all — the sinners and the righteous — and to each He will give what they have deserved, whether good or evil.

We can easily mislead people through deceit and lies; but God knows all our thoughts! He sees into the heart and everything is naked and open before Him!

With Him there is no respect of persons; <note>original: "geen aanzien van persoon" — an 18th-century expression meaning God does not show favoritism or partiality based on a person's social status, wealth, or title</note> the King and the Beggar, the rich and the poor are all equal in His eyes.

Woe to those who have done injustice and [did] not <meta>catchword: ver- (likely "verbeterd" or "verzoend," meaning "reformed" or "atoned")</meta>

Praise Him all birds in the sky, through your song! praise Him you fish in the water!

Praise Him you mountains and hills! praise Him you fertile fields, gardens, and trees!

You Kings and Princes of the earth, praise the Lord! you young men and young daughters <note>original: "jonge dochters," meaning young women or maidens</note>, praise His holy name!

Praise Him, you innocent children! you favorites of the Lord, praise the Lord!

May the name of the Lord be praised, from now until eternity!

And you, my soul! proclaim the praise of the Lord, as long as you shall live; all your sensations <note>original: "gewaarwordingen," referring to one's internal feelings, perceptions, or consciousness</note> must be praise, worship, thanks, and joy unto Him!

Glory be to the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit!

<vocab>Praise Him, Angels, firmament, Lord, eternity, Father, Son, Holy Spirit</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a lyrical call to worship, inviting all elements of creation—from celestial bodies and nature to people of all ages—to join in praising God and the Holy Trinity.</summary>

->*Prayer.*<-

> O God! all good and ill fortune, everything that happens to me, happens because You ordain and allow it so. I acknowledge and submit myself to your divine will. I adore You. I praise your divine Providence <note>The term "Providence" refers to God's intervention in the world and his proactive care for humanity.</note> in each and every circumstance. May your divine omnipotence <note>original: "almagt." Referring to God's unlimited power.</note> protect me! May your divine wisdom rule over me! May your divine mercy preserve and spare me! You can help me in all cases; You know how to help me. If I have not taken full refuge in **You**; if I have not always acted and thought according to these principles: then I regret this with all my heart. O heavenly Father! You care for me. O pour into my heart a childlike disposition <note>A "childlike disposition" implies a state of total trust and humility, similar to how a child trusts a parent.</note> toward **You**, so that I, filled with joy and trust, may not be wanton and proud in happy circumstances, nor too dejected in sad ones, but may always build upon **You**, continuing and concluding my short earthly life with the joyful expectation of a better eternal life, and may eternally laud and praise your divine Providence in heavenly glory. Amen.

<term>Omnipotence</term> (the quality of having unlimited or very great power), <term>Mercy</term> (compassion or forgiveness shown toward someone whom it is within one's power to punish), <term>Eternal life</term> (the Christian belief in a spiritual existence after death).

### After the Consecration.

->Earnestly, slowly.<- <note>original: "Met ernst, langzaam." These are musical or performative directions for the congregation.</note>

> So that Your suffering, Your goodness,
> May not be lost for us.

<term>The Consecration</term>: (original: "De Verandering") The central moment of the Mass when, according to Catholic doctrine, the bread and wine are changed into the Body and Blood of Christ.
<term>Savior</term>: (original: "Heiland") A traditional Dutch title for Jesus, emphasizing His role in the redemption of humanity.
<term>Sacrifice</term>: (original: "Offer") In this context, it refers to the Eucharist, which is viewed as a re-presentation of Christ's sacrifice on the Cross.
<term>Suffering</term>: (original: "Lijden") Refers to the "Passion"—the physical and spiritual agony of Jesus leading up to and during His crucifixion.
<term>Sinners</term>: (original: "Zondaars") The congregation identifies themselves as flawed humans in need of divine grace.

### The Spiritual Communion.

<note>A "Spiritual Communion" is a devotional prayer used by those who are unable to physically receive the Eucharist (the consecrated bread and wine) but desire to be united with Christ in spirit.</note>

->Melody as with the Offertory.<-

> O Lord! I am not worthy,
> To come to Your table;
> But You, make me worthy!
> Just as my soul’s wish is.
> O, still my longing,
> You bridegroom of souls!
> Let me receive You in Spirit,
> Here in Your sanctuary! (*repeat*).

<term>Lamb of God</term>: (original: "Agnus Dei") A title for Jesus referring to His sacrifice, based on ancient Jewish sacrificial traditions.
<term>Offertory</term>: (original: "Offertorium") The part of the Mass preceding the Consecration; the text indicates that the same tune should be reused here for simplicity.
<term>Bridegroom of souls</term>: (original: "zielenbruidegom") A poetic and mystical image common in 18th and 19th-century devotion, portraying the relationship between Jesus and the individual believer as a deep, spiritual marriage.
<term>Sanctuary</term>: (original: "heiligdom") Refers to the church building or specifically the area around the altar where the presence of God is believed to be most concentrated.

## HYMNS FOR A HIGH MASS.

<note>A "High Mass" (original: "Hooge Misse") is a more formal celebration of the Catholic liturgy where most parts of the service are sung by the priest, choir, or congregation, often accompanied by incense and additional ceremony.</note>

### At the Introit.

<note>original: "Introitus." This refers to the opening chant or hymn of the Mass, performed as the priest and assistants enter the sanctuary and approach the altar.</note>

->I.<-

> We cast ourselves down,
> Before You, God of Sabaoth!
> Hear our songs,
> As we, according to the commandment,

<term>Lamb of God</term>: (original: "Godslam") A title for Jesus used in the liturgy, signifying His role as a sacrifice for humanity's sins.
<term>Sabaoth</term>: (original: "Zebaoth") A Hebrew title for God meaning "Lord of Hosts" or "Lord of Armies," emphasizing His supreme majesty and power.
<term>Introit</term>: (original: "Introitus") The entrance rite of the Mass; the text here focuses on the physical act of kneeling in reverence at the start of the service.

### PRAYER AFTER HOLY MASS.

Dearest God! I thank You with all my heart that You have granted me the grace to attend this holy sacrifice. <note>original: "offer." In the Catholic tradition, the Mass is considered a "holy sacrifice" because it commemorates and makes present the sacrifice of Jesus on the cross.</note> Grant me also that I may be worthy of the enviable benefit that diligent Christians draw from it. — If I have not attended this sacrifice with the attention that such a holy ceremony deserved, forgive me this shortcoming for the sake of the merits of my Savior, who offered Himself to You in it, and grant that I may truly improve upon this shortcoming in the future. Amen.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a liturgical chalice holding a consecrated host (Eucharist) marked with a cross, surrounded by a radiant sunburst or halo of light.</image-desc>

<term>Prayer</term> (Dutch: *Gebed*); <term>Holy Mass</term> (Dutch: *Heilige Misse*); <term>sacrifice</term> (Dutch: *offer*); <term>grace</term> (Dutch: *genade*); <term>Savior</term> (Dutch: *Verlosser*); <term>Amen</term> (a Hebrew word meaning "so be it," used to conclude prayers).

<summary>This page concludes a series of hymns for the Mass with a plea for God's mercy at the Final Judgment and provides a closing prayer of thanksgiving and repentance for the worshipper to say before leaving the church.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, thanksgiving, Holy Mass, sacrifice, Last Judgment, grace, Savior, repentance</keywords>

### PRAYER after the HOLY MASS.

> Dear God, I thank You with all my heart
that You have granted me the grace to attend the 
Holy Mass. Lord, look upon
the adorable <note>In this historical context, 'adorable' carries the weight of the Latin *adorabilis*, meaning 'worthy of divine worship' or 'adoration.'</note> Sacrifice of Jesus Christ
with favor; though I have attended this Mass
and this sacrifice with but little of the attention
required by deep reverence, forgive me
therefore, Lord, for the merits of my Savior,
who is with You at this hour, and
give me the strength to substantially
improve in the future. Amen.

<note>The page ends with a small, faded decorative woodcut or vignette at the bottom center, appearing to depict a chalice or a small floral arrangement, common in 18th and 19th-century devotional printing.</note>
